| **[WebHarbor: Docking Real Websites for Evolving GUI Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03634v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GUI web agents require realistic and interactive environments that remain stable during both evaluation and training. Live website evaluation suffers from changing content, access restrictions, and limited coverage of authenticated workflows, while existing synthetic environments for training often lack functional depth and visual fidelity. We propose WebHarbor, a suite of 153 executable and dockerized website environments that faithfully reconstruct the visual interfaces, content, and functional workflows of real-world web services under deterministic control. We build WebHarbor through task-driven human-agent collaboration, where environments evolve as new tasks expand their functional scope, coding agents implement the required changes, and human contributors audit visual fidelity, functional correctness, and task validity. The resulting suite comprises 3,518 tasks spanning multi-step and post-authentication workflows, where existing works often fail to cover. Beyond reliable evaluation, WebHarbor provides high-fidelity synthetic web environments for cost-efficient reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate that a Qwen 4B-level GUI agent trained with RL in WebHarbor generalizes to unseen tasks and performs competitively with leading open-source GUI web agents across four benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/aiming-lab/WebHarbor.</p></details> | 31 pages |
| **[Drafting Agents, Not Just Tokens: Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16491v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Improving an agent through successive revisions requires balancing exploration and evaluation under a limited budget. A narrow search can miss useful sequences of edits, while fully evaluating every intermediate version often exhausts the budget before those sequences yield a verified improvement. Inspired by the draft--verify principle of speculative decoding, we propose Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement (SpecRSI), a two-level framework coupling inner-loop agent search with outer-loop improver learning. SpecRSI drafts successors before their predecessors are fully evaluated, while adopting only fully evaluated, admissible versions. In the inner loop, Verification-Aware Tree Search (VATS) allocates the budget between further edits and outstanding checks using the predicted verified return of a continuation. In the outer loop, Group Verification Optimization (GVO) updates the improver from completed searches while keeping the real evaluator fixed. We evaluate SpecRSI on scientific optimization (including active queue management (AQM) for network infrastructure), coding, and general agent-system benchmarks. Under matched evaluation-call budgets, SpecRSI improves scientific terminal gain over Janus by 60.0%--96.3% and area under the budget curve by 32.1%--91.6%, while achieving higher means across all 50 scientific backbone--task pairs and outperforming MetaRSI-v1 by 1.30--2.70 percentage points (pp) in system score across twenty backbones. On instance-level comparisons, it further improves PyBaMM terminal gain by 142.6% and SWE-Pro success by 6.0 pp at 7.0% and 9.2% lower token cost, respectively. These results demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed approach.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages; updated experiments, analysis, and figures; code available at https://github.com/SpecRSI-GPR/SpecRSI</p></details> |
| **[Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11435v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The growth of agent skills has transformed how agentic systems are built, evaluated, and deployed. As skill libraries continue to scale, rigorous evaluation becomes critical to ensuring their utility, quality, and safety in real-world applications. Consequently, the field is undergoing an emerging paradigm shift from isolated skill creation to automated, evaluation-driven skill evolution. In this survey, we systematically examine the landscape of skill evolution and evaluation beyond foundational skill creation. We categorize evolution into four distinct paradigms, spanning execution feedback, trajectory distillation, compression, and reinforcement learning, showing how each element contributes to improving skill utility and reliability. We also provide an analysis of six skill-centric benchmark categories, identifying structural gaps in benchmark coverage, trade-offs, and metric richness to advance skill research. Finally, we identify open directions for building skill ecosystems that are generalizable, efficient, and verifiably safe. The project URL is https://github.com/Cassie07/AgentSkill_Survey</p></details> |  |
| **[Strategist: Evolving Reasoning with Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03618v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model is only half of an AI agent system. The other half is the inference strategy: the reasoning procedure that turns model calls into a response. Today that second half is written by hand, and the experience of building one strategy never carries to the next. We introduce Strategist, a meta-agent that evolves inference strategies across tasks. Each strategy is written as a blueprint,executable code that other strategies can reuse, and together the blueprints form a library. Given a task and a small development set, Strategist retrieves related blueprints, writes candidate strategies, tests them on real samples, and revises them from the results. Every candidate then enters the library, so later tasks build on earlier discoveries. On 30 benchmarks, Strategist beats the strongest baseline on 24 and matches its average accuracy (74.7% vs.74.0%) at 39% lower inference cost. Its strategies also transfer to unseen tasks and other models without further search. The part of an AI system that is not improved on its own now does.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[HierFlow: Hierarchical Coupled Dual-Space Search for Automatic Agentic Workflow Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21609v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although structured workflows empower Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex problems, automating their creation is severely hindered by a vast combinatorial search space, frequently resulting in inflexible and resource-heavy offline training dependencies. To address this, we conceptualize workflow generation as an intertwined topology-and-execution search paradigm, where the broader topological layer dictates subtask boundaries and lower-level execution outcomes actively reshape the topology itself. Building on this foundation, we introduce HierFlow, a training-free, test-time hierarchical search architecture that automates agentic workflow design by merging feedback-guided topology adjustments with a fast, MCTS-inspired tree search for sub-workflow optimization. HierFlow maximizes efficiency through an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on contextual necessity, a mechanism we further support with an in-depth analysis detailing how varying degrees of cross-task coupling impact the effectiveness of hierarchical splitting. Comprehensive testing across question answering, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks confirms that HierFlow consistently outperforms strong baselines, delivering an optimal balance of high-quality results and computational efficiency without any additional training overhead.</p></details> |  |
| **[DiabAgent: Toward Agentic Physiological Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03601v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. Their ability to achieve effective physiological control through sustained intervention still lacks systematic quantitative evaluation. Type 1 diabetes provides a concrete setting: impaired insulin secretion creates dependence on exogenous insulin, with dosing continually adjusted using information about meals, exercise, illness, and glucose measurements. We introduce DiabAgent, a research suite for agentic physiological control comprising the Agentic Physiological Control (APC) framework, the DiabetSim simulator, and the SweetBench benchmark. APC controls glucose through medical-device tools, using ongoing observations to infer individual states, accumulate personal experience, and choose treatment actions. We build DiabetSim to simulate different patients, events, and device conditions for scalable virtual testing and training. Using DiabetSim, we construct SweetBench by instantiating 32 scenario designs as 212 glucose-control tasks. With Claude Opus 5.5 and a fixed decision interval of one hour, APC achieves a COGI of 73.5, compared with 71.2 for the open-source closed-loop baseline OpenAPS. Ablations further show that individual adaptation and contextual information improve glucose control. Combining high-frequency rule-based control with lower-frequency agent decisions shows potential for balancing control performance and computational cost. Our work provides a framework, simulator, and evaluation foundation for agents to participate in human physiological control.</p></details> |  |
| **[Four Bridges: Measuring Deception Propensity in a Multi-Agent Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03600v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they interact and coordinate with other agents, yet honesty is mostly measured in single-turn, single-agent benchmarks that pressure the model to deceive. We study deception that emerges in multi-agent interaction without any instruction to deceive. We introduce Kradle, a platform for evaluating AI models, and Four Bridges, an information asymmetry game in which four agents distribute across four rooms: one is the death room, the others hold food worth more if not shared. One agent, the informed agent, knows which room is the death room and can withhold or disclose that information. We evaluate eight frontier models as the informed agent. Deceptive behavior appears in every model and in 73% of all runs (6% to 100% per model), and does not decrease with capability tier within a model family. When the informed agent does disclose, the warning is often challenged, and agents that challenge it enter the death room twice as often as those that do not. Telling all agents that one of them knows the death room collapses deception in six of eight models; telling the informed agent that the room is permanently fatal moves only one. On an open-weight model, an honesty steering vector reduces deception to 0% and adversarial honesty training to 6%. The platform and game provide a testbed for measuring deceptive behavior and comparing interventions.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 14 figures, 16 tables</p></details> |
| **[Spacetime Emergence in the Memory of a Learning Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03596v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We show that the causal structure of spacetime can emerge within the memory of a learning agent performing a quantum information task. We train a model-based reinforcement-learning agent to maximize the quantum mutual information between localized quantum probes interacting with a quantum field in Minkowski spacetime, without directly providing it with information regarding the spacetime metric or causal structure. In learning to accomplish this task, the reinforcement-learning agent constructs an internal predictive model whose planner develops the characteristic null structure of spacetime, allowing us to reconstruct the causal structure of the Minkowski metric with high accuracy. Within this task, spacetime geometry is obtained from an agent's successful predictions and actions, providing a first step toward showing that spacetime concepts emerge from operational quantum experience.</p></details> | 19 pages, 7 figures |
| **[An Agentic AI Workflow for Liver Lesion Segmentation and Structured Radiology Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34599v3)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite advances in automated liver lesion segmentation, a gap remains between image-derived predictions and clinically interpretable radiology reports. Bridging this gap requires quantitative measurements that are both reproducible and traceable. TotalSegmentator-based nnU-Net models delineate the liver, Couinaud segments, and lesions, while computational tools extract volumes, dimensions, counts, and anatomical locations. A large language model (LLM) organizes these measurements into a predefined reporting schema, with numerical consistency checks against source measurements. On 51 LiTS cases, the segmentation stage achieved a liver Dice score of 0.947, lesion sensitivity of 75.6%, and positive predictive value of 68.9%. Exploratory screening of 25,494 MERLIN reports identified 10,208 (40.0%) with liver-related abnormalities. By connecting quantitative imaging evidence with agentic report generation, this work takes a step toward human-centered medical AI that prioritizes traceability and clinical relevance.</p></details> |  |
| **[FedReady: Bounded Agents for Federation Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03586v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) runtimes standardize aggregation and deployment, but each new study still requires federation formation: selecting task-eligible sites and preparing their local data and task code for federated training or analysis. This preparation recurs when the target changes, while raw records and site-private mappings are excluded from cross-site FL messages. We present FedReady, a system that performs federation formation with bounded LLM agents. Agents propose shared contracts, local adapters, and task code; the runtime owns workflow state and accepts artifacts only after checks tied to their digests. Candidate site discovery prioritizes recall; local eligibility verification checks task support before site admission. Once participating sites and artifacts are fixed, conventional federated training or analysis proceeds without readiness agents. Across 39 simulated retinal sites and four tasks, discovery reaches recall 1.00 at precision 0.65; local eligibility verification removes all 17 false positives without losing an eligible site-task pair. The participating sites supply 45,575 task-record instances and complete four 100-round jobs, including one with a runtime-generated contract. Across ten patient cohorts, preparation supports seven patient populations and holds endpoints at three sources as unsupported. Comparison with manually prepared reference populations and analysis (the manual reference) gives a maximum survival-curve difference of 0.001133. We also analyze 19 development failures, targeted classification ablations, and standalone and heterogeneous agent configurations. These results establish the feasibility of task-specific data preparation before ordinary FL training or analysis.</p></details> |  |
| **[TAMPAgent: Agentic Belief-Space Planning for Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03584v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-world robotic mobile manipulation requires acting in physical environments with only partial information. While recent Large Vision-Language Model (LVLM) agents show strong open-world reasoning capabilities, existing architectures that rely on direct tool calling struggle with geometric uncertainty and thus are limited to fully-observable tabletop environments. In contrast, Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) systems can solve long-horizon tasks under partial observability but incur high computational costs and require closed worlds. We propose \textbf{TAMPAgent}, an agentic planning framework that unifies the reasoning of LVLMs with the structured constraint satisfaction of TAMP systems. We endow an LVLM agent with a belief-space planning model operating over an extensible API of perception, planning, and control tools. The agent formulates a partial task plan composed of parameterized observation and execution actions, which is subsequently completed using classical search algorithms. After extracting the plan's geometric constraints, the agent leverages its tool API to bind valid parameter values, satisfying feasibility requirements and producing an executable motion plan. Upon execution, the agent continuously updates its belief history and repeats this closed-loop process by interleaving observation and manipulation actions. Evaluated across complex household mobile manipulation tasks on Rainbow Y1 and Unitree G1 platforms, TAMPAgent significantly outperforms direct tool-calling agentic baselines, demonstrating that integrating TAMP constraint satisfaction into agentic planning loops is essential for unstructured, partially observable environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website link: https://umishra.me/TAMPAgent/</p></details> |
| **[VeriHarm-Bench: Measuring Harmful Success in LLM Coding Agents Verifiably](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03575v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) coding agents increasingly operate with shell access, allowing them to write scripts, move files, alter permissions, and modify system configurations. However, an agent may achieve a user's goal while causing unintended harm, such as deleting unrelated files, weakening access controls, or corrupting data, in response to benign inputs without explicit jailbreaking. This failure mode is particularly important because apparent task success may reassure users that an agent behaved correctly, causing harmful side effects to go unnoticed. We call this harmful success: the agent successfully completes the intended benign task while simultaneously causing unintended harm. Despite its importance, harmful success is difficult to measure because conventional task-success metrics overlook harmful behaviors, while existing safety evaluations are often designed solely to assess harmful outcomes. We introduce VeriHarm, an automatic framework for constructing programmatically verifiable benchmarks that directly measure harmful success. VeriHarm minimally perturbs coding tasks to elicit unintended harmful behavior while maintaining the benignity of the task and preserving the original task objective. It then synthesizes executable harm tests that, together with the original task-correctness tests, evaluate both task accuracy and harmful outcomes. Frontier agents, including GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-Opus-5, achieve an average task accuracy of 95% on our curated VeriHarm-Bench, yet still struggle to complete tasks safely: their safe success rate, the fraction of tasks completed successfully without triggering the designated harm, averages only 37%. These results suggest that successful task completion can frequently coexist with unintended harm, and that task-success metrics alone may substantially understate the potential safety risks of coding agents.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[MCP Tool Poisoning in Agentic AI Frameworks: A Cross-Framework Measurement Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Model Context Protocol (MCP) lets agentic AI frameworks connect language models to external tool providers, but it carries attacker-controlled content (tool descriptions, schema fields, and response data) into model context without validation. Prior benchmarks measure model compliance with poisoned instructions without confirming real execution. We present the first cross-framework measurement of MCP tool poisoning with real tool execution. A 25-class root-cause taxonomy unifies prior classifications, and the open-source tool mcpvenom drives a 9,720-cell susceptibility matrix across 12 frameworks, 18 models, and 45 attack scenarios, with a dual oracle that confirms exfiltration via canary HTTP receipt. The overall attack success rate is 22.9% (2,113 vulnerable cells). Of these, 64.9% dispatch an attacker tool call, 23.0% contact the attacker endpoint, and 12.1% verifiably exfiltrate a secret. Resistance is model-level: nine core frameworks cluster within 23 to 26% (chi-squared = 3.30, p = 0.91), and no framework implements intentional defense. Incidental framework properties mitigate in specific cases, and a regex description scanner rejects 20 of 45 scenarios at a 7.2% false-positive rate, so metadata scanning alone is a partial control. Model ASR ranges from 0.4% to 49.7%. Claude Opus 4.8 is vulnerable in 8 of 530 cells while issuing 223 explicit refusals. A code-level audit finds no intentional defense across twelve frameworks, and all five coordinated disclosures across four vendors were rejected.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 19th Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security (AISec '26), co-located with ACM CCS 2026, The Hague, Netherlands. 12 pages, 3 figures, 9 tables; 4-page supplement included as ancillary file</p></details> |
| **[Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Requirement Discovery and Delivery for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03551v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM coding agents can increasingly automate model post-training, but whether they can act as forward-deployed engineer (FDE) agents, discovering customer requirements and turning them into deployment decisions, remains unclear. We study this on a real enterprise text-to-SQL engagement: a simulated customer, grounded in the real use case's requirements, starts with an ambiguous request. An agent must discover acceptance criteria, data permissions, and hardware constraints in the discovery phase, then plan and execute a post-training recipe in the delivery phase. Across 15 models, the strongest discover over 80% of withheld requirements and open-weight models discover 40-50%; in the delivery phase, only 25% of runs across eight coding-agent configurations pass all acceptance criteria. We identify four bottlenecks in this engagement: (1) incomplete discovery, with 40% of withheld requirements missing or misrepresented; (2) unreliable execution; (3) underused compute budgets and a lack of systematic comparisons across base models and post-training methods; and (4) benchmark accuracy does not guarantee production readiness. We suggest human review of discovered requirements, monitoring of the agent's plan during execution, and an explicit experiment-environment specification covering hardware, budget scope, and production checks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can LLM Agents Improve Imperfect Earth System Model Parameterizations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03543v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Earth system models (ESMs) rely on parameterizations to represent unresolved processes such as convection, cloud microphysics, and turbulence. However, translating advances in physical understanding into stable, validated parameterizations and integrating them into ESMs often takes years. Agentic AI could accelerate this process and enable more systematic exploration of design choices. Using warm-rain collision-coalescence as a test case, we evaluate agents' ability to synthesize scientific literature, reimplement published bulk parameterizations, and propose new functional forms as differentiable JAX code. We compare conventional parameterizations, data-driven emulators, and agent-generated closures against high-fidelity superdroplet simulations, repeating unattended experiments across ten language models. With expert guidance or iterative feedback, agents produced executable, differentiable, mass-conserving code, although unattended performance depended strongly on the scheme and language model. Iterative feedback lowered median rollout error for eight of ten models. Retuned physical schemes matched or outperformed many learned parameterizations at substantially lower computational cost, but two of 100 unattended agent-generated closures achieved slightly lower rain-mass error than the most accurate retuned scheme on held-out simulations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Sim2S...</summary><p>Sim2Sci Workshop, NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[An Agentic Framework for Automated Power System Studies and Grid Connection Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03534v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Connecting new generation and demand to the grid depends on two scarce skills: checking applications against a large, changing body of regulation, and running the network studies that decide where and how a project connects. We present Grid Agents, an AI system that brings both together, and the methodology we use to evaluate it. The Grid Ontology is its knowledge system. It represents the regulation, standards and engineering knowledge that grid work depends on, makes it searchable, and evolves as the agents learn from use. Every answer drawn from it rests on evidence the agents cite. Grid Studio is an agent system specialised for power-system engineering. It works in its own engineering environment with domain tools and a study framework, draws on the Grid Ontology, and relies on established solvers for every number it reports. In both, engineers make the decisions. We bring eight published power-system benchmark families, previously scattered across separate papers, into one suite of 519 tasks, and we verify not only each answer but the work behind it, by replaying the agent's tool calls. The Grid Ontology cites the right regulatory evidence more often than a general coding agent or retrieval pipelines, leads on LegalBench-RAG and ranks first on the public leaderboards of the Enterprise RAG Challenge and ViDoRe V3. Grid Studio scores highest on the 519 power-system tasks and is the fastest of all the systems we compare, both by a statistically significant margin. With an optimiser in the loop, its reinforcement packages cost 33-67% less on average than those the agent designs alone.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages for the main paper with 6 pages for Appendix</p></details> |
| **[DistDebug-Bench: Can LLM Agents Diagnose Bugs in Distributed Systems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03523v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents show strong potential in software engineering tasks such as competitive programming and code synthesis. Existing benchmarks, however, largely focus on small-scale settings such as self-contained Python modules, leaving distributed systems, an essential class of production software, under-examined. We introduce DistDebug-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating agents' ability to diagnose complex-system failures at the code level. It curates 120 high-quality real-world bugs from 11 widely used distributed systems, spanning 5 programming languages and 10 failure patterns. Through an extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs and agents, we identify substantial gaps between current agents and real-world debugging tasks. Benchmark: https://github.com/OrderLab/distdebug-bench. Website: https://distdebug.com</p></details> |
| **[Ask, Don't Equip: Question-Driven Coding Agents for Deep Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03517v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents for GPU kernel optimization sit at two ends of a structure-freedom tradeoff. Scripted agents fix what the model does at each step, via a learned policy, an optimization menu, or fixed roles, and are sample-efficient where the recipe applies and stall where it does not. Open-ended agents let a self-directed model mutate a population of kernels and pay for the freedom in trials: thousands of evaluations, repeated edits, and wrong candidates. Both respond to hard targets by equipping the agent with more: parsed profiler output, disassembly analysis, retrieval over documentation and libraries, and stores of lessons. We argue that both lack direction, a hypothesis about where the remaining gap is, and that a short question asked at the right moment supplies it without narrowing what the agent may do next. We present KernelSeek, a question-driven agent that makes an existing CUDA kernel faster with no operator-specific knowledge and no faster implementation to imitate: a general-purpose coding agent, a minimal harness that enforces a verifiable goal, and questions at decision points, first from a human, then from a bank the agent applies to itself. On the same task, evaluator, hardware, and week, a tool-rich autonomous agent built in the prevailing style plateaued at 0.50x FlashAttention-3 after 133 model-driven steps; KernelSeek reached 1.13x. It then produced dependency-free FlashAttention forward kernels for Hopper and Blackwell, single CUDA files with no CUTLASS, CuTe, cuBLAS, or cuDNN, correct on all 182 scenarios per architecture and 1.16x FlashAttention-3 and 1.46x FlashAttention-4 (geometric mean). Across 162 human messages and 11.5K agent tool calls, every large gain followed a question that moved the agent to a different layer of the system or rejected a claimed limit, never one that supplied information. We release the kernels, harness, question bank, and all traces.</p></details> |  |
| **[Optimize the Harness, Not the Model: Fitting a Minimal Agent to a Self-Hostable LLM for Investigative Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03434v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Government intelligence teams need LLM agents that can analyze large, messy evidence collections, but security and cost constraints often rule out hosted frontier models and broad, hard-to-audit tool surfaces. We report CLEO: Constrained Loop for Environment Optimization, a method for fitting a minimal agent harness to a fixed, self-hostable LLM. Given an expert-validated dataset, CLEO admits only the tools, guardrails, memory policies, and loop components that improve accuracy while remaining general and auditable. On our held-out dataset, the CLEO harness reaches an 83.8% pass rate, within 1.2 points of the strongest out-of-the-box Claude Code SDK baseline, while being two orders of magnitude smaller.</p></details> | <details><summary>3 pag...</summary><p>3 pages, 1 table. Industry Day talk at CIKM 2026, Rome, 7 November 2026</p></details> |
| **[DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Its 11 projects, spanning financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting, are reconstructed with 13 domain experts from real materials and chain 150 subtasks through 2-15 rounds of new materials and client feedback. Because no reference outputs are injected, agents inherit their own errors from one task to the next. Expert rubrics then score every checkpoint as well as the final package. Across seven model-harness systems, even the best system scores only 65.0 out of 100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. The failures are often failures of carrying work forward: in one audit project, all seven systems addressed the review findings, but only one preserved traceability. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery, which also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Reactive to Agentic Resilience: Multi-Timescale AI-RAN Orchestration Under Signaling Storms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03406v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Next-generation open radio access network (Open RAN) and AI-RAN deployments turn the RAN into a disaggregated, compute-aware platform whose service continuity depends on many control loops acting at different timescales. Resilience in this setting is best seen as a lifecycle spanning anticipation, absorption, adaptation, recovery, and evolution. Yet, existing mechanisms remain stage-specific and are rarely coordinated across the control hierarchy. This paper argues that agentic AI offers an orchestration layer that coordinates this lifecycle, turning operator intent into coordinated action while keeping slow language-model reasoning off the fast control path. Using mass-attachment signaling storms as a running study, we build a discrete-event Open RAN attach simulator that captures retry amplification, shared-compute contention, and non-instantaneous provisioning, and we propose a three-tier framework in which an orchestrator at the service management and orchestration layer sets a standing policy, a per-site non-real-time agent judges storms and anticipates scheduled crowds, and a deterministic near-real-time loop enforces every decision. Across experiments, the agentic controller approaches the resilience of a fully provisioned pool of servers at a quarter of its capacity by reading a scheduled event from a calendar and provisioning ahead of it. No reactive weight setting recovers this once the surge arrives. Free-text operator intents ground into control directives at 94% exact match on previously unseen intents, and a single instruction reshapes live behavior end to end. We also quantify the latency, cost, and contention limits that bound it. The supplementary material provides analytical proof of the derived Theorem on anticipation horizon, as well as the subsequent Corollary results on reactive provisioning limits.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 Pa...</summary><p>13 Pages, 9 Figures, 7 Tables. Paper submitted to IEEE for possible publication</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking AI in Medical Imaging: Assumptions, Reality, and Reframing; Towards Physician-Centered Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27428v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical imaging has served as primary proving ground for clinical AI, yet a decade of intense research has not translated into proportionate bedside impact. This work identifies 8 interconnected dimensions of this misalignment: 1) the dominance of pixel-only models in a multimodal clinical world; 2) the erosion of physician trust through opaque and inflexible systems; 3) the unfulfilled promise of foundation models in data-sparse medical domains; 4) the persistent bottleneck of non-shareable under-curated datasets; 5) the gap between validated algorithms and deployable clinical platforms; 6) the slow, unmonitored translation of evidence into routine practice; 7) the risk of AI displacing rather than extending physician judgment; and 8) the failure of prediction-centric AI to generate actionable, agentic clinical guidance. For each dimension, we reframe the problem and propose a path forward, culminating in a vision of physician-centered AI solutions with agentic reasoning that extend, rather than replace, clinical judgment.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 3 figures. If lifetime allocations sum to at most one, a weighted Ville argument bounds the probability of ever promoting a rule whose declared bounded net score has always had nonpositive conditional mean. The result permits dependent candidates, adaptive births, and indefinite monitoring. We audit the implementation in two settings. The market replay produces zero promotions after costs. The certificate controls historical false promotion under the declared null; it is not a certificate that an earlier edge remains profitable at deployment. AI-use disclosure. Generative AI tools, including OpenAI Codex, assisted manuscript preparation. Our approach achieves a gap-type F1 of 0.831 (vs. 0.533 for the strongest baseline) and remediation P@5 of 0.872 (vs. 0.635 for retrieval methods), demonstrating promising potential for automated compliance workflows. The structured agent coordination reveals a path toward human-in-the-loop compliance systems where automated gap extraction reduces expert burden while maintaining auditability, and the taxonomy-driven design enables extension to emerging regulatory frameworks beyond</p></details> |  |
| **[From Local Skills to Long-Horizon Web Tasks: Progressive Skill Exploration for LLM Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03116v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are driving autonomous agents toward real-world environments, with the Web emerging as one of the most representative interaction scenarios. Under SP, per-output token averaging sets each role's gradient share, a hidden hyperparameter absent from single-agent RL, and the solving roles learn slowly; reweighting it gives SP the speed and peak of IP, and its decline. | **[StateFence: Task-State Protection Against Evidence-Chain Contamination in Blue-Team LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02605v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Indirect prompt injection creates a distinctive risk for blue-team Large Language Model (LLM) agents in Security Operations Centers (SOCs). | **[Organizational Learning in Materials Discovery Campaigns: Which Databases, Calculations, and Agent Teams Pay Off](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02571v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>More databases can make a materials campaign worse. Through an organizational lens, we trace each source's contribution. One band-gap campaign ranks seven anion families from archived measured gaps, one vote per source. In the first half of its budget, all seven pooled rank worse (rank agreement 0.737; 1 = measured order) than the best pool of three (0.851). As a stability check, choosing the pool on half the runs and testing on the rest kept the gap. Splitting Materials Project afterwards points to its transition-metal oxides and fluorides. Two databases from one pipeline add nothing over one, but pooling gives their shared ordering two votes. New calculations repay their cost early only when two or three campaigns reuse them. Flat language-model agent teams trailed a random-pick baseline (mean 0.876, sd 0.096, 20 searches). An adaptive Al-Mg-Si team, mostly interatomic potentials, was scored on unseen HSE06 calculations. In its first quarter of compute it found 49% of the ten lowest-formation-energy compounds, against at most 40% for fixed teams and 73% for MACE-MP-0 alone. Merging members whose orderings agreed carried most of its gain. | **[CR-Bench-v2: Evaluating the Real-World Utility of AI Code Review Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02566v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid growth of automated code generation has created a pressing need for automated code review. We propose two complementary metrics: \emph{Known-Defect Detection Rate} (KDDR), which measures whether an agent identifies the labeled defect, and \emph{Usefulness Rate}, which measures the fraction of review comments that are actionable. | **[EEFA: A Framework for the Ethical Evaluation of AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02561v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid deployment of autonomous AI agents across consequential domains including healthcare, legal services, nancial management, and enterprise workows has created an urgent governance challenge. While the eld of AI evaluation has produced sophisticated benchmarks for measuring agent capability, these frameworks measure exclusively what agents can do rather than how they do it. The ethical dimensions most critical to responsible agent deployment, in- cluding respect for human autonomy, harm avoidance, transparency and non-deception, privacy compliance, and accountability, are systematically absent from current evaluation practice. This paper addresses that gap through three contributions. First, a structured literature review of twelve leading AI agent benchmarks characterizes the ethical dimensions those bench- marks do and do not measure. Third, a reproducible validation protocol is specied for applying the EEFA to deployed agent systems, with illustrative test scenarios across all ve framework dimensions. The EEFA is organized across ve primary dimensions: Autonomy Preservation, Harm Avoid- ance, Transparency and Non-Deception, Privacy Compliance, and Accountability and Traceabil- ity. The framework maps directly onto the requirements of the EU AI Act and the NIST AI Risk Management Framework, positioning it as a practical compliance assessment instrument for high-risk AI applications. | **[When Cache Pressure Becomes a Trap: Metastability in Agent Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02543v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents repeatedly send a growing conversation to a model, making prefix reuse central to efficient serving. On SGLang, a ten-minute surge reduced goodput by about 88% over the following hour. Removing the residual burst sessions restored service within minutes. The sustaining mechanism is population-cache feedback. Slower calls prolong sessions and enlarge their aggregate context, or working set; the resulting cache misses increase recomputation and further delay departures. Across 36 simulated deployments, it flags all 17 cache-first cases. Once the cache collapses, low-reuse departure capacity sets the scale of the load reduction needed for recovery. Guided by these dynamics, we build Basin, which gives each session a fixed priority, reserves their predicted peak contexts against an adaptive admission budget, extends the cache with idle decode memory, and sheds excess demand. On real H200 1P3D deployments under surges, including long-context SWE-bench Pro sessions, an arrival-time prototype achieves 1.20--1.70x the goodput of Continuum and 2.35--3.59x that of HiCache alone. | **[Scientific Liquidity Agents: An Agent-Based Model of Knowledge Arbitrage in Scientific Communities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02539v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific knowledge gains new uses when ideas, methods, and results move between research contexts. SLA represents domain specialists and epistemic arbitrageurs within a shared environment of research problems, researcher interactions, and scientific artifacts. Individual decisions connect to collective state through a belief-desire-intention architecture, a prerequisite-based problem graph, a dynamic interaction network, and a persistent publication registry. The framework makes researcher roles and knowledge-transfer frictions explicit, providing a process-oriented account of scientific production and exchange. Two exploratory simulations with 10 and 18 agents produce 68 and 274 registered papers. In the smaller run, arbitrageurs take part in 16 of the 26 contact pairs that join this community into one connected component, while topical similarity alone splits it into seven. The same run yields three similarity edges, 26 contact pairs, and two coauthorship pairs, so measured community structure depends on what counts as a link. Paper production is abundant, but no recorded replication succeeds, which places the bottleneck in verification rather than production. Solved-problem sets correspond to the prerequisite reachability of the disciplines represented in each community. SLA makes knowledge liquidity, the movement and reuse of artifacts across domains, a quantity that agentic science can simulate and measure.</p></details> | <details><summary>First...</summary><p>First version on Zenodo, December 8, 2025: https://zenodo.org/records/17861999</p></details> |
| **[AMLBench: A Real-Incident Benchmark for Agentic Blockchain Anti-Money Laundering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02530v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tracing illicit funds on public blockchains remains largely manual. To bridge this gap, we present AMLBench, an anti-money laundering (AML) benchmark built from 24 real-world cases and 5.05 million Ethereum transfer records. For each case, agents start from limited clues and explore these transfer records through composable transaction-query and fund-flow analysis tools, deciding which accounts and branches to pursue. With GPT-6 Astra, AMLAgent achieves macro averages of 80.82% transfer-level precision and 84.73% query coverage across 24 real-world cases. Further analysis of the large-scale Bybit case reveals substantial differences in transfer retention and token usage across models, even with similar submission precision. AMLBench establishes a reproducible benchmark for evaluating both tracing outcomes and investigative processes, laying the groundwork for more reliable and efficient autonomous AML investigations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Touching Projected Agents through the DAT Screen -System Design and Questions of Interaction Boundaries-](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02525v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Touching a virtual agent involves more than detecting a finger on a display: the contact surface can deform and resist the user's push, and the agent can respond to that action. We describe the tension-controlled DAT implementation and its demonstrations, distinguishing what has been observed from what remains an open design question. | **[Cassette-Editor: An Agent Harness for Editing Video the Way You Do](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37690v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents work in an environment built for them: they read and edit a project, a compiler or a test checks each change automatically and points to the fault, and version control keeps every change in a project that people can review, revert, and continue. Video editing agents lack such an environment; whether an edit is correct shows only in the rendered video, which is costly to inspect and does not point to the decision at fault. Existing systems give the agent either a set of editing operations, which covers only anticipated edits, or a script whose output is a video the user cannot keep editing. We present Cassette-Editor, a complete agent harness for video editing that lets the agent edit the way a person does: knowing what the project allows, seeing what each change affects, and revising from there. It makes the timeline document the agent's action language, in which edits are compositions of typed changes rather than separate tools. A schema check and a rulebook shared with manual editing define what the project allows. An accepted patch commits as one step of the history the user also edits; a rejected one leaves the project unchanged, with each problem located and repair options listed for the next attempt. Reads map source analysis to the frames of the current edit, so the agent can tell what the edit now shows. A parity evaluation shows that every manual action the editor registers, on its test fixtures, can be expressed as an agent patch that reaches the same independently specified document. In a preliminary case study of consecutive revisions to a long documentary rough cut, Cassette-Editor completed more of the judged requests than Codex CLI, which worked from media files without the project, with the same model at similar cost; with a stronger model, under which both completed every request, it used fewer tokens and less time per delivery.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 7 figures, 4 tables. v2: substantially revised, with a new title, rewritten text, and a new evaluation. | **[Cumulative and Audited Guardrail Tool Evolution for LLM Agent Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02516v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate in open-world environments where safety risks cannot be fully predefined. Inspired by how human societies transform experiences into transferable protective knowledge, EVOLVE externalizes security knowledge into executable guardrail tools that are continuously inherited, independently audited, and iteratively refined through lifelong interaction. | **[Benchmark of THReD: Evaluating an LLM Agent and Its Preprocessing Tools for Turning Electronic Health Records into AI-Ready Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02484v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly built into data-analysis software, yet little evidence shows how reliably they preprocess clinical data or whether the tools they invoke give correct, reproducible output.
